Tradition notes
The Authorized (King James) Version, commissioned by King James I for the Church of England and first published in 1611; the 1769 Blayney standardized text is the basis of this edition. Long the standard English Bible across Protestant traditions.

William Wilberforce (1759-1833) was an English politician and evangelical reformer, best known for the abolition of the British slave trade. A Practical View (1797) is his manifesto of Evangelical Anglicanism, contrasting nominal Christianity with genuine conversion. A bestseller across the English-speaking world.

William Carey (1761-1834) was an English Particular Baptist minister who became the founder of modern Protestant missions. This pamphlet (1792) argued systematically that the Great Commission applies to all Christians in all ages, catalysing the formation of the Baptist Missionary Society. Foundational document of Protestant missiology.

Tradition notes
John Henry Newman (1801–1890) wrote the Apologia in 1864 as a defense of his religious integrity after Charles Kingsley's public attack. It traces his theological development from his evangelical Anglican upbringing through the Oxford Movement to his 1845 conversion to Roman Catholicism. Regarded as one of the greatest spiritual autobiographies in the English language.

Provenance notes
Contains all 23 Stations plus Death's Duel sermon appended. Each Station parsed as a section with three child sub-sections (Meditation, Expostulation, Prayer). OCD devotional schema was considered but requires month/day integer fields; these Stations are illness-stage-keyed, so structured_text used instead. Plain-text inline emphasis markers are stripped in the JSON projection; bare numeric note anchors are tagged as [[pg-note-anchor:N]].

1631
Author id
donne-john
Era
post-reformation
Original language
en
Tradition notes
John Donne (1572–1631), Dean of St Paul's Cathedral, wrote the Devotions during a severe illness in 1623. The work's 23 Stations trace his sickness, recovery, and spiritual reflection — each in three movements: Meditation, Expostulation, and Prayer. The seventeenth Meditation contains the famous 'No man is an island' passage.

Provenance notes
Books I-IV combined from two PG volumes. Book III chapters are split across both volumes and merged here. Source hash is SHA-256 of the concatenation of both file hashes. Plain-text inline emphasis markers are stripped in the JSON projection; bare numeric note anchors are tagged as [[pg-note-anchor:N]].

Tradition notes
Calvin's Institutes (1536, final Latin edition 1559) is the foundational systematic theology of the Reformed tradition. This PG edition uses the John Allen translation (6th American edition, Philadelphia: Presbyterian Board of Publication). The original task specification anticipated the Beveridge 1845 translation; however, PG #45001 and #64392 contain the Allen translation. Both are public domain.

Tradition notes

Provenance notes
Parser strategy: extend gutenberg_systematics.py rather than fork. Hooker matches the existing multi-volume structured_text parser shape; its extra Keble apparatus is handled by work-specific book ranges and cleanup. The plain-text OCR does not expose stable chapter title lines, so numbered Roman divisions and running-header chapter signals are normalised into chapter nodes. Keble editorial footnotes and marginal notes remain inline where OCR interleaves them; sermons and the tractate on Justification are outside this Polity-only output. Plain-text inline emphasis markers are stripped in the JSON projection; bare numeric note anchors are tagged as [[pg-note-anchor:N]].

Henry Drummond (1851-1897) was a Scottish Free Church minister and scientist. The title essay (on 1 Corinthians 13) was first delivered c.1884 and became one of the most widely circulated Christian pamphlets of the 19th century. This PG edition includes the main essay plus five additional addresses.

Provenance: source type
internet_archive_djvu
Tradition notes
Edward Fisher was an English lay theologian whose Marrow of Modern Divinity (1645) became central to Scottish Federal Theology after Thomas Boston (1677-1732) reissued it with annotations in 1718. The 1720s 'Marrow Controversy' in the Church of Scotland turned on Boston's reading of Fisher's covenantal framework. This is the canonical Scottish Reformed form of the work.

Tradition notes
Boethius (c. 480-524) was a late Roman philosopher and statesman whose Theological Tractates address speculative theology: the Trinity, divine predication, ontology of the good, Catholic faith, and Christology (against Eutyches and Nestorius). Translated by H.F. Stewart and E.K. Rand (1918) for the Loeb Classical Library.

Author birth year

Author death year
1888
Tradition notes
Keil was a conservative Lutheran Old Testament scholar; Delitzsch a Lutheran Hebraist and strong supporter of Jewish evangelism. Their commentary is known for rigorous philological and exegetical scholarship, engagement with the Hebrew text, and defence of the historical reliability of the OT. Delitzsch later developed more irenic views toward critical scholarship. OT-only commentary.

Tradition notes
Clarke was a leading Methodist theologian and itinerant preacher under John Wesley's influence. His commentary reflects Wesleyan-Arminian soteriology and is notable for its philological depth -- Clarke was a proficient Hebrew and Greek scholar. Contrasts sharply with Gill in its anti-Calvinist sympathies.

Tradition notes
Gill was the pastor of Southwark Baptist Church (London) for over 50 years and a leading Calvinistic Baptist theologian. His commentary is notable for extensive engagement with Rabbinic and Patristic sources. Often called hyper-Calvinist in soteriology, though this is debated.

Author death year
1714
Tradition notes
Henry was a Welsh Nonconformist (Presbyterian) minister, strongly influenced by Puritan theology. His commentary is the most widely used devotional/practical commentary in the Reformed tradition. Published 1706-1714; Genesis through Acts completed by Henry, Romans through Revelation finished posthumously by colleagues from his notes.

Tradition notes
Wesley was the founder of Methodism and represents the Arminian tradition in Protestant Christianity. His notes were written as a practical guide for lay Bible readers, not as a scholarly commentary. OT notes drew heavily on Matthew Poole and Matthew Henry; NT notes are more original, drawing on Bengel's Gnomon Novi Testamenti.

Tradition notes
J.B. Lightfoot (1828-1889) was a Church of England bishop and one of the Cambridge Triumvirate of NT scholars (with Westcott and Hort). His commentaries combine rigorous textual criticism with orthodox Anglican theology.

Provenance notes
Sourced from JWBickel/BibleDictionaries on HuggingFace. Underlying text is public domain (19th century). Structured JSONL format by JWBickel; license confirmation pending (EMAIL-4 sent). The 2026-07-16 full field census found only term and definitions in every record; no explicit alternate-term field is present. Bible citations embedded in definitions are mapped to scripture_references through the shared OSIS normalizer, and explicit See/See also headword assertions are mapped to related_terms. alt_terms remains empty because the source does not provide that apparatus. Observed populated fields: scripture_references on 3964/4560 entries (12235 citation groups); related_terms on 123/4560 entries (144 links).

Tradition notes
Smith was an English Anglican classical scholar. The dictionary reflects broad evangelical Protestant scholarship of the mid-19th century.

Tradition notes
The 1662 Book of Common Prayer is the foundational liturgical text of the Church of England, authorised under the Act of Uniformity (1662). Its collects draw on older pre-Reformation sources, the 1549/1552 BCPs of Thomas Cranmer, and earlier Latin sacramentaries. build/parsers/bcp1928.py@v1.1.0
Provenance notes
The 1928 BCP is unambiguously in the public domain (published 1928, all authors long deceased). The source site (episcopalnet.org) has no robots.txt. A 2-second courtesy crawl delay is used between page requests. 100 individual HTML pages cover the full propers (Collects, Epistles, and Gospels) for all Sundays, Holy Days, and special occasions. Good Friday has three collects on a single page; these are extracted as separate prayer records. Exception: the Trinity23 and Trinity24 pages on episcopalnet.org contain hymn suggestions only -- no collect text. The collects for these two Sundays were sourced from the 1928 US BCP scan on Internet Archive (https://archive.org/details/1928bookofcommon0000prot, pages 222-223, fetched 2026-04-01). Note: the 1928 US BCP text differs from the 1662 CoE BCP on these two collects (Trinity23 uses a semicolon after "godliness"; Trinity24 uses "for the sake of Jesus Christ" rather than "for Jesus Christ's sake").
Schema type
prayer

post-reformation
Original language
en
Tradition notes
The 1928 Book of Common Prayer served the Episcopal Church (USA) from 1928 until 1979. Its collects draw on older pre-Reformation sources, Thomas Cranmer's 1549/1552/1662 BCPs, and revisions adopted by the General Convention. This dataset covers the Sunday, Feast Day, and special occasion collects from the Collects, Epistles, and Gospels section.

Tradition notes
Lancelot Andrewes (1555–1626), Bishop of Winchester, wrote the Preces Privatae as a personal devotion book in Greek, Latin, and Hebrew. The Newman 1840 translation (Tract for the Times lxxviii) is the most accessible English version. Andrewes is the father of Caroline Anglicanism and one of the principal translators of the King James Bible.

Tradition notes
Alexander Maclaren (1826-1910) was a Baptist minister who served at Union Chapel, Manchester, from 1858 to 1903. His Expositions of Holy Scripture (serialised 1892-1910, Hodder & Stoughton) covers most of the Bible in detailed verse-by-verse expository sermons. Maclaren was president of the Baptist World Alliance in 1905. His theology is broadly evangelical and Reformed, emphasising scripture, grace, and the believer's union with Christ.

Tradition notes
Martin Luther (1483-1546) was the primary figure of the German Reformation. His Church Postil (Kirchenpostille) comprises sermons on the lectionary texts for the church year, originally preached 1521-1522. The Lenker translation (1903-1909) covers both Epistle sermons (vols 7-9 of 'Precious and Sacred Writings') and Gospel sermons / Church Postil (vols 10-14).

Tradition notes
John Henry Newman (1801-1890) was an Anglican clergyman at St. Mary the Virgin, Oxford, who later converted to Roman Catholicism (1845). These 8 volumes of sermons, preached 1825-1843 and published 1834-1843 (Rivingtons), are widely regarded as the most influential Victorian Anglican sermon series. The 1868 Copeland edition (repr. Rivingtons) is used here.
